Dimension-free bounds in high-dimensional linear regression via error-in-operator approach
Fedor Noskov, Nikita Puchkin, Vladimir Spokoiny

Abstract
We consider a problem of high-dimensional linear regression with random design. We suggest a novel approach referred to as error-in-operator which does not estimate the design covariance Σ \Sigma directly but incorporates it into empirical risk minimization. We provide an expansion of the excess prediction risk and derive non-asymptotic dimension-free bounds on the leading term and the remainder. This helps us to show that auxiliary variables do not increase the effective dimension of the problem, provided that parameters of the procedure are tuned properly. We also discuss computational aspects of our method and illustrate its performance with numerical experiments.



1
Introduction

Recent advances in supervised machine learning devoted to understanding of deep neural networks revealed surprising effects going beyond the classical statistics theory. In contrast to the standard intuition that a learner should search for a trade-off between approximation and estimation errors, researchers empirically observed that large interpolating rules may still have small test error. Moreover, when the number of parameters exceeds sample size the prediction risk of neural networks passes a U-shaped curve and decreases again
(
Zhang et al., 2017
;
Nakkiran et al., 2020
)
. A bit later it became clear that benign overfitting and double descent are not distinctive features of deep learning. Similar phenomena are ubiquitous for overparametrized models such as random forests and random feature models
(
Belkin et al., 2019
;
Mei and Montanari, 2022
)
, kernel methods
(
Belkin et al., 2018
;
Liang and Rakhlin, 2020
)
, and linear regression
(
Bartlett et al., 2020
;
Hastie et al., 2022
)
to name a few.
In
(
Belkin et al., 2018
)
, the authors reasonably suggested that we must study more tractable “shallow” methods better before diving into deep learning theory.

In the present paper, we consider a classical linear regression problem, where a learner aims to estimate an unknown vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
from i.i.d. pairs
\{({\bf X}_{i},Y_{i}):1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
generated from the model

Y_{i}={\bf X}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}+\varepsilon_{i},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

(1)

We do not impose any assumptions on the ambient dimension
d
(in particular, it may be much larger than
n
) or on the structure of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}
, but we require
\mathbb{E}\|{\bf X}_{1}\|^{2}
to be finite. Given an independent copy
({\bf X},Y)
of
({\bf X}_{1},Y_{1})
, the quality of an estimate
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is measured with its quadratic risk

\displaystyle R(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{({\bf X},Y)}(Y-{\bf X}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{({\bf X},Y)}(Y-{\bf X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})^{2}+\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\right\|^{2},\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma=\mathbb{E}{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

We can rewrite (
1
) in a more compact vector form

{\bf Y}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},

(2)

where
{\bf Y}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}
, and
\mathbb{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
is a matrix with columns
{\bf X}_{1},\dots,{\bf X}_{n}
. The problem (
2
) provides a convenient framework for studying complex high-dimensional phenomena. For this reason, it has recently got considerable attention.
Researchers put a lot of efforts into careful and subtle analysis of popular minimum norm least squares and ridge regression estimates defined as

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(LS)}=(\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top})^{\dagger}\,\mathbb{X}{\bf Y}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}=(\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top}+\tau I_{d})^{-1}\mathbb{X}{\bf Y},

respectively. Here and further in the paper,
B^{\dagger}
stands for the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of a matrix
B
.
In
(
Bartlett et al., 2020
;
Chinot and Lerasle, 2025+
;
Chinot et al., 2022
)
, the authors relied on concentration of measure and statistical learning theory and established dimension-free rates of convergence for
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(LS)}
depending on the eigenvalues of
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}{\bf X}_{1}{\bf X}_{1}^{\top}
rather than on the ambient dimension
d
. In the same spirit,
Tsigler and Bartlett (2023)
derived non-asymptotic dimension-free bounds on the squared prediction risk of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
and supported observations of
Kobak et al. (2020)
that the optimal value of the penalization parameter
\tau
may be negative due to the implicit regularization. Another line of research (see, for instance,
(
Dobriban and Wager, 2018
;
El Karoui, 2018
;
Taheri et al., 2021
;
Richards et al., 2021
;
Mahdaviyeh and Naulet, 2019
;
Derezinski et al., 2020
;
Hastie et al., 2022
;
Wu and Xu, 2020
;
Bach, 2024
)
) examined the least squares and ridge regression estimates through the lens of random matrix theory and studied
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(LS)}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
in the so-called proportional regime, that is, when the ratio
d/n
tends to some constant
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
as
n
approaches infinity. We would like to emphasize that the aforementioned approaches complement each other. While
Bartlett et al. (2020)
argue that
\mathbb{E}\|{\bf X}_{1}\|^{2}={\rm Tr}(\Sigma)=o(n)
,
n\rightarrow\infty
, is a necessary condition for benign overfitting, in the proportional regime one usually assumes the opposite:
{\rm Tr}(\Sigma)=\Omega(n)
. However, in a recent paper
(
Cheng and Montanari, 2024
)
, the authors combined resolvent analysis with concentration inequalities and obtained non-asymptotic risk expansion for the ridge regression estimate.

In the context of overparametrized models, explicit regularization has a very nice property previously unseen in the classical underparametrized setup. In
(
Nakkiran et al., 2021
)
, the author noticed that optimally regularized least squares estimate can mitigate double descent. This fact is of great importance, because in the presence of double (or, more generally, multiple descent, see, for instance,
(
Chen et al., 2021
;
Meng et al., 2024
)
) adding new batches to the training data may lead to worse generalization error (see
(
Nakkiran et al., 2020
, Section 7)
).
In the present paper, we study properties of an estimate with double regularization. Let us note that both
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(LS)}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
have a form
(\breve{\Sigma})^{\dagger}{\bf Z}
, where
{\bf Z}=\mathbb{X}{\bf Y}/n
and
\breve{\Sigma}
is an estimate of
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}{\bf X}_{1}{\bf X}_{1}^{\top}
. Instead of estimating
\Sigma
directly, we incorporate learning of
\Sigma
into an optimization problem. To be more specific, we introduce an objective function

{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})={\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)=\frac{1}{2}\|{\bf Z}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-A\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\|\widehat{\Sigma}-A\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2},

(3)

where

{\bf Z}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}{\bf Y},\quad\widehat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top},

and consider a regularized empirical risk minimizer

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{A})\in\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}\limits_{(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)}{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A).

(4)

Similar ideas were used in linear inverse problems
(
Hoffmann and Reiss, 2008
;
Trabs, 2018
)
and Bayesian inference
(
Spokoiny, 2023
)
.
In the context of kernel ridge regression,
Chen et al. (2023)
replaced the standard Euclidean distances
\|x-x^{\prime}\|
by
\|\Sigma^{1/2}(x-x^{\prime})\|
and considered a family of Gram matrices parametrized by
\Sigma
. However, this is very different from our setup, because, in contrast to
(
Hoffmann and Reiss, 2008
;
Trabs, 2018
;
Chen et al., 2023
)
, we do not assume any low-dimensional parametrization of the operator
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. This means that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
estimates a parameter
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ},\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ},\Sigma)
of much larger dimension than
d
. At the first glance, we dramatically increased the problem complexity. However, let us recall that we are interested in the target parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
rather than in the whole vector
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d+d^{2}}
. In the simple case
\mu=+\infty
, it is straightforward to check that the estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
corresponds to the plug-in one

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\infty}=(\widehat{\Sigma}^{2}+2\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\widehat{\Sigma}{\bf Z},\quad\text{where}\quad{\bf Z}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}{\bf Y}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}{\bf X}_{i}^{\top},

which is nothing but the ridge regression estimate applied to the model

{\bf Z}=\widehat{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}+{\bf U},\quad\text{where}\quad{\bf U}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}.

(5)

Hence, if we take the parameter
\mu
sufficiently large, the rate of convergence of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
will not suffer (though a rigorous proof of this fact is quite challenging). Moreover, additional regularization with respect to operator helps
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
to be less susceptible to double descent. Following
(
Spokoiny, 2023
)
, we call
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}

error-in-operator estimate
.

Contribution.

The main contributions can be summarized as follows.

•

We suggest a novel approach for the problem of random design linear regression. We specify leading terms in the bias and variance expansions of the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and derive an explicit high-probability upper bound on the remainder. In contrast to the standard ridge regression estimate,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
does not have a simple closed-form expression. For this reason, we have to use more sophisticated tools for its analysis.

•

We establish non-asymptotic dimension-free bounds on the excess quadratic risk
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})-R(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})=\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|^{2}
and show that the influence of the introduced operator
A
is negligible once the parameter
\mu
is tuned properly.

•

We discuss minimization the non-convex objective (
3
) and prove that a simple alternating optimization procedure converges to the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

•

We illustrate the validity of statistical properties of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
with numerical experiments. We also show that the generalization error of the error-in-operator estimate is smaller than the ones of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\infty}
and that the risk of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is less susceptible to double descent.

Notation.

Throughout the paper,
\|B\|_{\rm F}
and
\|B\|
stand for the Frobenius and the operator norm of
B
, respectively. If the matrix
B
is symmetric positive semidefinite and
B\neq O
, where
O
is the matrix with zero entries, we denote its effective rank by

{\mathtt{r}}(B)={\rm Tr}(B)/\|B\|.

For any matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times s}
, their Kronecker product
A\otimes B
is a matrix of size
pr\times qs
, defined as

\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}B&\dots&a_{1q}B\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{p1}B&\dots&a_{pq}B.\end{pmatrix}

For any matrix
U\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
with columns
{\bf u}_{1},\dots,{\bf u}_{q}
, the vectorization operator is given by
{\rm\bf vec}(U)=({\bf u}_{1}^{\top},\dots,{\bf u}_{q}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{pq}
. Here and further in the paper, the bold font is reserved for vectors, while matrices and scalars are displayed in regular font. The operator
\nabla_{A}
stands for the gradient with respect to
{\rm\bf vec}(A^{\top})
. Thus, if
{\bf a}_{1},\dots,{\bf a}_{d}
are the rows of
A
and
f(A)
is a smooth function, then
\nabla_{A}f=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{{\bf a}_{1}}f^{\top},\dots,\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{{\bf a}_{d}}f^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
.
For a random variable
\xi
and a random vector
\boldsymbol{\nu}
, their Orlicz
\psi_{s}
-norms,
s\geqslant 1
, are defined as

\|\xi\|_{\psi_{s}}=\inf\left\{t>0:\mathbb{E}e^{|\xi|^{s}/t^{s}}\leqslant 2\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\boldsymbol{\nu}\|_{\psi_{s}}=\sup\limits_{\|{\bf u}\|=1}\|{\bf u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\nu}\|_{\psi_{s}}.

The expressions
(a\vee b)
and
(a\wedge b)
denote
\max\{a,b\}
and
\min\{a,b\}
, respectively.
Sometimes, instead of the standard
{\mathcal{O}}
notation, we use
f\lesssim g
or
g\gtrsim f
, which mean that there is a universal constant
c>0
, such that
f\leqslant cg
.

Paper organization.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we present our main results concerning statistical properties of the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. In Section
3
, we discuss computational aspects of optimizing the non-convex objective (
3
). We illustrate performance of the suggested procedure with numerical experiments in Section
4
. Sections
5
and
6
are devoted to the proofs of bias and variance expansions of the estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
. Derivations of numerous auxiliary results are moved to appendix.

2
Statistical properties of the error-in-operator estimate

In the present section, we discuss main statistical properties of the error-in-operator estimate (
4
). In particular, we specify leading terms in expansion of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and provide an upper bound on its quadratic risk.
We assume that the covariates
{\bf X},{\bf X}_{1},\dots,{\bf X}_{n}
meet the following requirements.

Assumption 2.1
.

There exists a positive constant
C_{X}
such that, for any matrix
M
and any
\lambda
satisfying the inequality
C_{X}|\lambda|\leqslant 1/\|M\|_{\rm F}
, it holds that

\log\mathbb{E}\exp\left\{\lambda(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})^{\top}M(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})\right\}\leqslant\lambda{\rm Tr}(M)+C_{X}^{2}\lambda^{2}\|M\|_{\rm F}^{2},\quad\text{where}\quad\Sigma=\mathbb{E}{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\top}.

In
(
Puchkin and Rakhuba, 2024
)
, the authors imposed the same assumption on the distribution of
{\bf X}_{1},\dots
,
{\bf X}_{n}
in the problem of covariance estimation, and an equivalent condition appeared in
(
Puchkin et al., 2025
)
. We refer a reader to these two papers for a comprehensive list of distributions satisfying Assumption
2.1
. In particular, it is fulfilled when
{\bf X}
has a Gaussian distribution or when
\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X}
has independent sub-Gaussian components. Such conditions often appear in the context of high-dimensional linear regression (see, for instance,
(
Bartlett et al., 2020
;
Nakkiran et al., 2021
;
Chinot and Lerasle, 2025+
;
Bach, 2024
)
). More interesting examples include situations when
{\bf X}
has a convex concentration property, like in
(
Cheng and Montanari, 2024
)
, or when the underlying distribution meets the logarithmic Sobolev inequality (such as the uniform distribution on a sphere
(
Meng et al., 2024
)
). Finally, Assumption
2.1
holds in nonlinear feature models of the form
{\bf X}=\varphi(W\boldsymbol{\xi})
, where
\varphi
is a Lipschitz map and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
has either a standard Gaussian distribution
(
Hastie et al., 2022
)
or the uniform distribution on a unit sphere
(
Mei and Montanari, 2022
)
(the distribution of
{\bf X}
should be considered conditionally on
W
). This follows from the fact that
\boldsymbol{\xi}
with Gaussian or uniform distribution on a sphere satisfies the logarithmic Sobolev inequality. Then, due to the Herbst argument, it possesses a Lipschitz concentration property. This yields that
{\bf X}
has the same property as well, and, hence, it satisfies the Hanson-Wright inequality (see
(
Adamczak, 2015
)
). Let us note that it is enough to check Assumption
2.1
for symmetric matrices only. Indeed, due to the convexity of the squared Frobenius norm, we have

\left\|0.5M+0.5M^{\top}\right\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leqslant 0.5\|M\|_{\rm F}^{2}+0.5\|M^{\top}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=\|M\|_{\rm F}^{2},

and then it holds that

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\exp\left\{\lambda(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})^{\top}M(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})\right\}

\displaystyle=\log\mathbb{E}\exp\left\{\lambda(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})^{\top}(0.5M+0.5M^{\top})(\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X})\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda{\rm Tr}(0.5M+0.5M^{\top})+C_{X}^{2}\lambda^{2}\|0.5M+0.5M^{\top}\|_{\rm F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda{\rm Tr}(M)+C_{X}^{2}\lambda^{2}\|M\|_{\rm F}^{2}.

Assumption
2.1
means that the vector
\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X}
is sub-Gaussian and fulfils the
\psi_{2}
-
L_{2}
-equivalence condition (see
(
Puchkin and Rakhuba, 2024
, Lemma B.2)
) often arising in high-dimensional statistics. However, we would like to mention that in
(
Puchkin et al., 2025
, Proposition 2.3)
the authors gave an example of a random vector satisfying the
\psi_{2}
-
L_{2}
-equivalence requirement, which does not meet Assumption
2.1
. We also impose the following condition on the noise distribution.

Assumption 2.2
.

The i.i.d. pairs
\{({\bf X}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}):1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}
are such that

\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{1}\,|\,{\bf X}_{1})=0,\quad\mathbb{E}{\bf X}_{1}{\bf X}_{1}^{\top}=\Sigma,\quad\text{and}\quad\|\Sigma^{-1/2}{\bf X}_{1}\varepsilon_{1}\|_{\psi_{1}}=\sigma<+\infty.

For instance, Assumption
2.2
holds if
\varepsilon_{1}
is Gaussian (or, more generally, sub-Gaussian) and independent of
{\bf X}_{1}

(
Hsu et al., 2012
;
Nakkiran et al., 2021
)
. However, we do not need independence of
\varepsilon_{i}
from
{\bf X}_{i}
for our purposes.

Two main results of this section are devoted to bias and variance of the estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
defined in (
4
). For this reason, let us define a population counterpart of the objective (
3
) given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\Sigma\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-A\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\|\Sigma-A\|_{\rm F}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}

and introduce the best parametric fit

\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*},A^{*})\in\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}\limits_{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}).

(6)

One of the key properties of the functional
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
we will often exploit in our analysis is that its gradient differs from
\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
by a parameter-free vector
\mathfrak{z}
:

\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})=\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})-{\bf\mathfrak{z}},\quad\text{where}\quad{\bf\mathfrak{z}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z}\\
{\rm\bf vec}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d+d^{2}}.

In other words, for any
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}
the difference
{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})-{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
is an affine function of
({\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z})
and
(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)
. We are ready to present bias and variance expansions of the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
be non-negative. Assume that the following inequalities hold:

\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\frac{\mu}{420},\quad\text{and}\quad\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\|\Sigma\|\leqslant\frac{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}{168}.

Then the vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
defined in (
6
) satisfies

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}-{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|\leqslant 210\left(\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}+\frac{\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)\left\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right\|,\quad\text{where}\quad{\bf b}_{\lambda}=-\lambda(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}.

Theorem 2.4
.

Grant Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
.
Let us fix an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,1)
. Suppose that
4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)
and
2^{12}(1+C_{X})^{2}({\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta))
do not exceed the sample size
n
. Assume that the parameters

\mu\geqslant 112\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
and
\lambda>0
satisfy the inequalities

\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\frac{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}{8\cdot 18\cdot 56},\quad 5\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|(\sqrt{\lambda}\land\|\Sigma\|),

(7)

17(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\sqrt{\lambda},

(8)

and

\mu\sqrt{\lambda}\geqslant 395\cdot 8\sigma\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},\quad\sqrt{\lambda}\geqslant 395\cdot 8C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

(9)

Then, with probability at least
(1-13\delta/2)
, the vector

\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\Sigma{\bf U}-\Sigma(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma){\bf b}_{\lambda}-(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Sigma{\bf b}_{\lambda}\quad\text{with}\quad{\bf U}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\bf X}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}

satisfies the inequality

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\left(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{19\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3/4}}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\Psi(n,\delta)}{27\lambda}}\right)\Psi(n,\delta)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)},

where

\displaystyle\Psi(n,\delta)=196\left(\frac{\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}.

The proofs of Theorem
2.3
and Theorem
2.4
are postponed to Sections
5
and
6
, respectively. These results ensure that

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\approx{\bf b}_{\lambda}+(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\left(\Sigma{\bf U}-\Sigma(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma){\bf b}_{\lambda}-(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Sigma{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right)

(10)

with high probability provided that the sample size
n
and the penalization parameter
\mu
are large enough. It is not surprising that the leading terms in the expansion (
10
) correspond to the ones in decomposition of the estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\infty}
defined as

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\infty},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{\infty})\in\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}\limits_{(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta})}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\|{\bf Z}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-\widehat{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}\right\}.

Hence, the insertion of the operator
A
has no negative influence on the quadratic risk
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})
provided that the parameter
\mu
is sufficiently large. Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
essentially reduce the analysis of the excess risk
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})-R(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})=\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|^{2}
to large deviation bounds on the right-hand side of (
10
), which easily follow from the next theorems.

Theorem 2.5
.

Grant Assumption
2.1
and let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times d}
be arbitrary matrices. Let us fix any
\delta\in(0,1)
satisfying the inequality

{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}A^{\top}A\Sigma^{1/2})\,{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}B^{\top}B\Sigma^{1/2})+\log(2/\delta)\leqslant 4n.

Then, with probability at least
(1-\delta)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|B(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)A^{\top}\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|A\Sigma^{1/2}\|\,\|B\Sigma^{1/2}\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}A^{\top}A\Sigma^{1/2})\,{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}B^{\top}B\Sigma^{1/2})+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}.

Theorem 2.6
.

Suppose that a sample
\{({\bf X}_{i},\varepsilon_{i}):1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
consists of i.i.d. pairs of random elements satisfying Assumption
2.2
.
Let us fix an arbitrary matrix
B\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times d}
and any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}B^{\top}B\Sigma^{1/2})+\log(2/\delta)\leqslant n.

Then, with probability at least
(1-\delta)
, it holds that

\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}B{\bf X}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\right\|\leqslant 8\sigma\left\|B\Sigma^{1/2}\right\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma^{1/2}B^{\top}B\Sigma^{1/2})+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}.

The proofs of Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
are moved to Appendix
E
. They rely on the PAC-Bayesian variational inequality (see, e.g.,
(
Catoni and Giulini, 2017
, Proposition 2.1)
). In
(
Zhivotovskiy, 2024
;
Puchkin and Rakhuba, 2024
;
Abdalla and Zhivotovskiy, 2025+
)
, the authors use similar arguments in the context of covariance estimation. Let us note that the upper bound in Theorem
2.5
agrees with the results of
Bunea and Xiao (2015)
and
Puchkin et al. (2025)
in the case
A=B=I_{d}
and with Theorem 16 of
Hsu et al. (2012)
when
A=B=(\Sigma+\lambda I_{d})^{-1/2}
.

Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
applied to the vector
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
from Theorem
2.4
help us to quantify the excess risk of the error-in-operator estimate in terms of

r_{q}(k)=\sum\limits_{j>k}\left(\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sigma_{k+1}}\right)^{q},\quad k\in\{1,\dots,d\},

(11)

where
\sigma_{1}\geqslant\sigma_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\sigma_{d}
denote the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
. In Appendix
F
, we prove the following upper bound on the norm of
\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})
with explicit constants.

Corollary 2.7
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
2.3
and Theorem
2.4
. Let
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d}
stand for the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
and define

k^{*}=k^{*}(\lambda)=\max\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\sigma_{k}^{2}\geqslant 2\lambda\right\}.

(12)

Fix an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

\left(1+\frac{2\lambda}{\|\Sigma\|^{2}}\right)^{2}\big(k^{*}+r_{4}(k^{*})\big)+\log(4/\delta)\leqslant n\quad\text{and}\quad k^{*}+r_{2}(k^{*})+0.25\log(4/\delta)\leqslant n

(13)

with
r_{2}(k)
and
r_{4}(k)
given by (
11
). Then, with probability at least
(1-8\delta)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right\|+4\left(2\sigma+C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{k^{*}+r_{4}(k^{*})+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2C_{X}\|\Sigma^{3/2}{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\sqrt{2\lambda}}\sqrt{\frac{4k^{*}+4r_{2}(k^{*})+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}+\diamondsuit,

(14)

where
{\bf b}_{\lambda}=-\lambda(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}
and

\displaystyle\diamondsuit

\displaystyle=210\left(\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}+\frac{\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\left\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right\|+\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{19\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3/4}}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\Psi(n,\delta)}{\lambda}}\right)\Psi(n,\delta).

(15)

Corollary
2.7
gives an intuition for the choice of
\mu
. It should be chosen in such a way that
\diamondsuit={\mathcal{O}}(1/\mu)
,
\mu\rightarrow\infty
, does not exceed the leading terms in the risk bound (
14
).
We would like to note that the expression in the right-hand side of (
14
) can be simplified in view Lemma
G.1
from Appendix
G
below. Let

\Sigma=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{j}{\bf v}_{j}{\bf v}_{j}^{\top}

be the eigenvalue decomposition of
\Sigma
. For any
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, we define
\beta_{k}={\bf v}_{k}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}
and the projections of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
onto the linear span of
{\bf v}_{1},\dots,{\bf v}_{k}
and its orthogonal complement, respectively:

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\leqslant k}^{\circ}=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k}\beta_{j}{\bf v}_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\theta}_{>k}^{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\leqslant k}^{\circ}.

Then, under the conditions of Corollary
2.7
, with probability at least
(1-8\delta)
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\sigma_{k^{*}}^{2}\left\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}_{\leqslant k^{*}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}_{>k^{*}}\right\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(1+8C_{X}\sqrt{\frac{k^{*}+r_{2}(k^{*})+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\right)

\displaystyle+8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{k^{*}+r_{4}(k^{*})+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}+\diamondsuit,

(16)

where
\diamondsuit
is defined in (
15
). The first term in the right-hand side of (
16
) corresponds to the bias and the random design effect while the second one reflects the noise influence. It is worth mentioning that the variance of the standard ridge regression estimate

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}=\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}\limits_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left\{\left\|{\bf Y}-\mathbb{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|^{2}+\tau\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}\right\}

(17)

is of order (see, for instance,
(
Hsu et al., 2012
;
Cheng and Montanari, 2024
;
Bach, 2024
)
)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}\,\big|\,\mathbb{X}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{{\rm Tr}\big(\Sigma^{2}(\Sigma+\tau I_{d})^{2}\big)}{n}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\widetilde{k}+r_{2}(\widetilde{k})}{n}\right),\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

(18)

where

\widetilde{k}=\widetilde{k}(\tau)=\max\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\sigma_{k}\geqslant\tau\right\}.

Remark 2.8
.

To be more precise, in
(
Cheng and Montanari, 2024
;
Bach, 2024
)
, the variance is expressed through the effective regularization parameter
\varkappa(\tau)
. In the present paper, we focus on the case
{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{n})=o(n)
(see Theorem
2.4
). In this situation,
\varkappa(\tau)
and
\tau
do not differ too much. In contrast,
Cheng and Montanari (2024)
are interested in the opposite scenario.

Let us note that
\widetilde{k}(\tau)
coincides with
k^{*}(\lambda)
(see (
12
)) when
\tau^{2}=2\lambda
. This suggests us to compare the performance of the standard ridge estimate (
17
) with the prediction risk of the error-in-operator estimate (
4
) with
\lambda=\tau^{2}/2
. First, observe that the bound (
16
) yields that

{\rm Var}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\big|\,\mathbb{X}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{k^{*}+r_{4}(k^{*})}{n}+\diamondsuit^{2}\right),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

Since
r_{q}(k)
monotonously decreases in
q>0
,
{\rm Var}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\,\big|\,\mathbb{X}\right)
exhibits a better behaviour than
{\rm Var}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}\,\big|\,\mathbb{X}\right)
. At the same time, the first term in the right-hand side of (
16
) is of the same order as the upper bound on the bias of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
obtained in
(
Cheng and Montanari, 2024
, Proposition 2.2)
. Moreover, one can easily show that the leading bias term
\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|
,
\lambda=\tau^{2}/2
of the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
does not exceed
\sqrt{2}\tau\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\Sigma+\tau I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
(see Lemma
G.2
).

3
Computational aspects

Since the objective (
3
) of the error-in-operator estimate (
4
) is non-convex, we must elaborate on computational aspects of the suggested procedure. We defined the estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
as a minimizer of a multivariate polynomial of degree
4
. Optimizing a multivariate fourth-degree polynomial is intractable in general. Therefore, to find
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we should harness the inner structure of our problem. First, we reduce the inference to a convex optimization problem. Let us fix an arbitrary
\rho_{0}
from
(0,1/3]
and define

\Upsilon(\rho_{0})=\left\{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}=(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A):(1-\rho_{0}^{2})\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}\leqslant\rho_{0}^{2}\mu^{2},\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-A\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}\leqslant\rho_{0}^{2}\mu^{2}\lambda\right\}.

(19)

The introduced set plays a crucial role in our analysis, because, the function
{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)
has a positive definite Hessian on
\Upsilon(\rho_{0})
(see Lemma
A.1
below).
However, note that
\Upsilon(\rho_{0})
is not convex. For this reason, we restrict our attention on its convex subset of the form (see Proposition
H.4
)

\displaystyle\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}\subset\Upsilon(\rho_{0}),

where

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu\;\text{and}\;\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\leqslant 5\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/96\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}

\displaystyle=\left\{\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-{\bf Z}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/3\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:\|A\|\leqslant 3\|\Sigma\|+\sqrt{\lambda}/3\right\}.

Next, we show that under mild assumptions the estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
belongs to
\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Grant Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
and fix an arbitrary
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
n\geqslant 2^{12}(1+C_{X})^{2}\left({\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)\right)
. Let
\rho_{0}\leqslant 1/8
and let the sets
\Theta
,
\mathsf{H}
,
\mathsf{A}
be as defined above. Assume that the non-negative parameters
\lambda
and
\mu
satisfy the inequalities

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\frac{\rho_{0}\mu}{7},\quad

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\frac{\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}{56\cdot 18},\quad\frac{2^{11}\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\frac{\lambda}{\|\Sigma\|}\land\sqrt{\lambda},

and

\frac{2^{13}(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\lambda.

Then,
(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*},A^{*})\in\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}
and, with probability at least
(1-\delta)
, the triplets
({\bf 0},{\bf Z},\widehat{\Sigma})
and
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}},\widehat{A})
belong to the set
\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}
as well.

Remark 3.2
.

Careful examination of the proof of Lemma
3.1
reveals that one can replace Assumption
2.1
with a bit milder
\psi_{2}
-
L_{2}
-equivalence condition.

Lemma
3.1
implies that
{\mathtt{L}}(\cdot)
possesses local convexity property, which is widely used in non-convex optimization (see the survey
(
Jain and Kar, 2017
)
). Thus, instead of (
4
), we can study the problem

\displaystyle{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)\rightarrow\min_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A}\quad\text{ subject to }(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)\in\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}.

(20)

The problem (
20
) could be solved using constrained convex optimization methods, if the sets
\Theta,\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{A}
were observable. Unfortunately, it is not the case, but we prove below that the following procedure converges to
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}},\widehat{A})
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}

\displaystyle\in\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta},(A_{t-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}+{\bf Z})/2,A_{t-1}),

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle\in\operatornamewithlimits{argmin}_{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}}{\mathtt{L}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t},(A\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}+{\bf Z})/2,A)

The estimates
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}
and
A_{t}
admit explicit expressions, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
can be computed as

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\frac{1}{2}({\bf Z}+\widehat{A}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}).

We summarize the optimization procedure in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Error-in-operator regression

1:

observations
{\bf Y}
, feature matrix
\mathbb{X}
, regularization parameters
\mu,\lambda>0
, number of iterations
T
.

2:

Initialization: set
{\bf Z}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}{\bf Y}/n
,
\widehat{\Sigma}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}/n
, and
A_{0}=\widehat{\Sigma}
.

3:

for

t=1,\dots,T

do

4:

Compute

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}=(A_{t-1}^{\top}A_{t-1}+2\lambda I_{d})^{-1}A_{t-1}^{\top}{\bf Z}

5:

and

A_{t}={\bf Z}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}^{\top}\left(2\mu^{2}I_{d}+\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{-1}+\widehat{\Sigma}\left(I_{d}+\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}^{\top}}{2\mu^{2}}\right)^{-1}.

6:

end

for

7:

return

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{T}
.

The following theorem justifies convergence of Algorithm
1
to the error-in-operator estimate
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
defined in (
4
).

Theorem 3.3
.

Grant Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
. Fix arbitrary positive numbers
\delta<1
and
\rho_{0}\leqslant 1/8
. Suppose that
n\geqslant 2^{12}(1+C_{X})^{2}\left({\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)\right)
and assume that the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
satisfy the conditions

\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu/7,\quad\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/(56\cdot 18),

(21)

\lambda\geqslant\frac{2^{13}(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}},

(22)

and

\frac{\lambda}{\|\Sigma\|}\wedge\sqrt{\lambda}\geqslant\frac{2^{11}\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

(23)

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, Algorithm
1
outputs
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{T}
such that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{T}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}^{2(T-1)}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|+8\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\,\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{\lambda n}}\right).

Let us note that larger values of
\mu
yield weaker conditions (
21
)–(
23
) on admissible
\rho_{0}
.
This means that the algorithm converges faster as
\mu
increases. This is the behavior one should expect, because in the case
\mu=\infty
the objective
{\mathtt{L}}(\cdot)
can be minimized explicitly in one step.

4
Numerical experiments

In this section, we illustrate our studies with numerical experiments. First, we show that Theorems
2.3
,
2.4
present correct leading terms in the expansion of
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})-R(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})
. We fix the following parameters of the random design linear regression problem:

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=200,

\displaystyle{\bf X}_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(k^{-1/8}\cdot\sin(\pi k\cdot\xi_{k})\right)_{k=1}^{d},

(24)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=k^{-3},\quad k=1,\ldots,d,

where
\xi_{k}
are i.i.d. samples from
\operatorname{Uniform}[-1;1]
. It is clear, that
\Sigma
is diagonal with eigenvalues
k^{-1/4}/2
,
k=1,\ldots,d
. We set
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}
to be i.i.d. Gaussian random variables with the standard deviation
0.09
.

For this set of parameters, we plot the dependence of ratio
\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|/\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|
on
\mu,\lambda
in Figure
1
(left). As implied by Theorem
2.3
, this ratio is close to
1
for large values of
\mu
.

To illustrate Theorem
2.4
, we fix
\mu=10^{8}
. We denote

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}.

If Theorem
2.4
yields the correct leading term of
\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})
, then the ratio
\|\Sigma^{1/2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|/\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\|
should be close to
1
for large enough
n
. We plot the dependence of
\|\Sigma^{1/2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|/\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\|
on
\lambda
and
n
in Figure
1
(middle and right). To obtain smoother curves, we averaged the studied ratio over
40
generations. On the right picture,
\lambda_{opt}(n)
is estimated by the grid search for each
n
. As one can see, our experiments agree with suggested theory.

Then, we compare the performance of our estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
and the ridge estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
defined in (
17
). We estimate the optimal hyperparameters
\mu,\lambda
and
\tau
by the grid search. Due to (
16
), the variance leading term
(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d})\boldsymbol{\zeta}
of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
depends on the fourth powers of covariance eigenvalues, while the performance of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
depends on the second powers. Thus, we expect that our estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
should outperform
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
for
\Sigma
with heavy-tailed eigenvalues. We support these observations by Figure
2
(left plot). A reader can observe that our estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
outperforms the ridge estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{}^{(R)}
for the suggested setup (
24
).

Next, we study the impact of finite
\mu
on the performance of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
in the considered setup (
24
). Empirically, we show that finite
\mu
can smooth the double descent curve. Figure
2
(right) displays the dependence of the risk
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})-R(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})
on the sample size
n\in\{50,100,\ldots,500\}
. For each
n
and
\mu=\infty
, we find
\lambda_{opt}(n)\in\{1.3^{-40},1.3^{-39},\ldots,1.3^{39}\}
by the grid search. Then, for each
n
and
\lambda\in\lambda_{opt}(n)\cdot\{10^{-6},10^{-4},10^{-2},1,2\}
, we estimate
\mu_{opt}(\lambda,n)
by the grid search on
\{2^{0},2^{1},\ldots,2^{29}\}
. We plot the dependence of the excess risk
R(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}})-R(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})
on
n
and
\lambda
for
\mu=\infty
by the dashed line. The solid line corresponds to
\mu=\mu_{opt}(\lambda,n)
. We did not obtain the impact of finite
\mu
on the risk curve when
\lambda\geqslant\lambda_{opt}(n)
, but finite
\mu
can significantly mitigate the effect of double descent if
\lambda<\lambda_{opt}
.

Figure 1:
Left:
The ratio between the bias component
\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|
of the risk and its leading term established in Theorem
2.3
.
Middle and Right:
The ratio between the variance component of the risk
\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})\|
and its leading term established in Theorem
2.4
.

Figure 2:
Numerical studies of Error-in-Operator estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
.
Left:
The comparison of the Error-in-Operator estimator and the standard ridge estimator.
Right:
The effect of finite
\mu
on the double descent curve.

5
Proof of Theorem
2.3

We start with studying the difference
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}
. For any
t\in[0,1]
, let us define
{\bf u}^{(t)}=(1-t)\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}+t\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
. Due to the Newton-Leibniz formula, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ})=\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}({\bf u}^{(1)})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}({\bf u}^{(0)})=\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}({\bf u}^{(t)})(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ})\,{\rm d}t.

Let us denote
H({\bf u})=\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}({\bf u})
and
\left\langle H\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}H({\bf u}^{(t)}){\rm d}t
for brevity. Generally, for a matrix-valued function
f({\bf u})=f(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)
, we let

\displaystyle\left\langle f\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}f({\bf u}^{(t)}){\rm d}t.

We are going to apply Lemma
B.1
with
\rho_{0}=1/7
. To satisfy its conditions, we bound
\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
as follows:

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/(7\cdot 24)=\frac{\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}{24}.

(25)

Then, Lemma
B.1
and Lemma
A.1
imply that the matrix
\left\langle H\right\rangle
is invertible. With the introduced notations we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}=\left\langle H\right\rangle^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ})).

Since
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
minimizes
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
, we obtain that

\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ})=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}).

It is straightforward to check that the only non-zero components of
\nabla{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ})
correspond to the first
d
coordinates. For this reason, we can restrict our attention on
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}
.
Let

\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi}=\begin{pmatrix}\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{\eta}\boldsymbol{\eta}}&\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\boldsymbol{\eta}A}\\
\left\langle H\right\rangle_{A\boldsymbol{\eta}}&\left\langle H\right\rangle_{AA}\end{pmatrix}

stand for the block of
\left\langle H\right\rangle
corresponding to the nuisance parameter
\chi=(\boldsymbol{\eta},A)
.
Due to the block-inversion formula (
53
), we have

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}=(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}))=-\lambda(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ},

(26)

where
(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})
is the Schur complement of the submatrix
\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi}
. Rearranging the terms, we obtain that

\displaystyle\big\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}-{\bf b}_{\lambda}\big\|

\displaystyle=\big\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}+\lambda(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\big\|

\displaystyle=\lambda\left\|\left[(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})-I_{d}\right](\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda\left\|(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})-I_{d}\right\|\|(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|

(27)

\displaystyle=\left\|(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})-I_{d}\right\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|

The next lemma ensures that the rightmost expression is small compared to the norm of
\lambda(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}
. For the sake of brevity, let us define
\rho=7\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|/\mu
. By the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\rho\leqslant 1/60.

(28)

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
hold. Let
\mathfrak{m}(\cdot)
be an arbitrary probability measure supported on
[0,1]
. Define

\displaystyle\left\langle H\right\rangle^{\,\mathfrak{m}}=\int_{0}^{1}H((1-t)\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}+t\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}){\rm d}\mathfrak{m}(t).

Then it holds that

\left\|(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}(\left\langle H\right\rangle^{\,\mathfrak{m}}/\left\langle H\right\rangle^{\,\mathfrak{m}}_{\chi\chi})-I_{d}\right\|\leqslant 15\rho+\frac{5\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}.

Let us denote
(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})^{-1}(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})-I_{d}
by
E
.
Applying Lemma
5.1
with
\mathfrak{m}
equal to the uniform measure on
[0,1]
, we obtain that

\|E\|\leqslant 15\rho+\frac{5\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}\leqslant 15\rho+\frac{5\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2},

where we used
\rho\leqslant 1/60
and (
25
).
It yields

\displaystyle\left\|(\left\langle H\right\rangle/\left\langle H\right\rangle_{\chi\chi})^{-1}(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d})-I_{d}\right\|

\displaystyle=\|(I_{d}+E)^{-1}-I_{d}\|=\|E(I_{d}+E)^{-1}\|\leqslant\frac{\|E\|}{1-\|E\|}

\displaystyle\leqslant 30\rho+\frac{10\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}.

Substituting this bound into (
27
), we get that

\big\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}-{\bf b}_{\lambda}\big\|\leqslant 30\rho\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|+\frac{10\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|^{2}}{\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}.

\square

6
Proof of Theorem
2.4

Before we dive into details, let us discuss the main idea of the proof. We split it into four major steps. First, we check that the conditions of Lemma
3.1
are satisfied with high probability. This yields that both
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
and
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
belong to a convex set around
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{\circ}
where, according to Lemma
A.1
, the Hessian
\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
is positive definite. Hence, if the penalization parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
are chosen in agreement with (
7
), the difference
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})
can be approximated by the standardized score
H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
where
H_{*}
stands for
\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})
(see Lemma
6.1
). On the second step, we show that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
admits an expansion with a leading term
{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
equal to the first
d
components of
H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
. After that, we quantify the remainder
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})
handling the subvectors of the standardized score corresponding to the target and the nuisance parameters separately (see Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.3
, respectively). Summing up the bounds obtained on the second and the third steps, we provide an upper bound of the form

\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\|\lesssim\lambda^{-3/4}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\Psi(n,\delta)}{27\lambda}}\right)\Psi(n,\delta)

(29)

holding with high probability. Finally, on the fourth step we elaborate on
{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
and argue that it is close to the vector
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
defined in the statement of Theorem
2.4
.

Step 1: localization.
To start with, let us define

\rho_{0}=\frac{7\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\left(1\vee\frac{72\|\Sigma\|}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right).

Note that
\rho_{0}\leqslant 1/16
, because of the condition (
7
) and the inequality
\mu\geqslant 112\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
. Moreover, (
7
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{2^{11}\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\frac{2^{11}\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\lambda}}{7\cdot 72\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{5\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\lambda}}{\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|(\sqrt{\lambda}\land\|\Sigma\|)=\sqrt{\lambda}\land\frac{\lambda}{\|\Sigma\|},

while (
8
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{2^{13}(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\rho_{0}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{13}(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\lambda}}{7\cdot 72\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 17(1+C_{X})\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\lambda}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{4{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(2/\delta)}{n}}\leqslant\lambda.

This means that the assumptions of Lemma
3.1
are satisfied. Hence, there is an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
of probability at least
1-\delta
, such that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
and
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
belong to
\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}
on
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=\left\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu\text{ and }\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\leqslant 5\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/96\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}

\displaystyle=\left\{\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{\eta}-{\bf Z}\|\leqslant\rho_{0}\mu\sqrt{\lambda}/3\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:\|A\|\leqslant 3\|\Sigma\|+\sqrt{\lambda}/3\right\}.

In the rest of the proof, we restrict our attention on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
.

Step 2: expansion of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
.
The result obtained on the previous step allows us to exploit geometric properties of
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
. To be more precise, Proposition
H.4
implies that

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}},\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}\in\Theta\times\mathsf{H}\times\mathsf{A}\subseteq\Upsilon(\rho_{0})\quad\text{on ${\mathcal{E}}_{0}$,}

where the set
\Upsilon(\rho_{0})
is defined in (
19
).
Then, due to Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
A.3
,
\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
is positive definite and smooth on the segment
\{t\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}+(1-t)\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1\}
. These properties play a crucial role in the proof of the following technical result.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
S
,
{\mathtt{D}}
, and
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}
be
(2d+d^{2})\times(2d+d^{2})
block-diagonal matrices of the form

S={\rm diag}\big(\Sigma^{1/2},I_{d+1}\otimes O_{d}\big),

{\mathtt{D}}_{*}^{2}={\rm diag}\left((A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d},2I_{d},I_{d}\otimes\left(\mu^{2}I_{d}+\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}\right)\right),

and

{\mathtt{D}}_{0}^{2}={\rm diag}\left((A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d},2I_{d},I_{d}\otimes\left(\mu_{0}^{2}I_{d}+\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top}\right)\right),

(30)

where
\mu_{0}\in(0,\mu]
is an arbitrary constant.
Fix an arbitrary
\rho_{0}\in(0,1/16]
and consider an event
{\mathcal{E}}
such that
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}
belong to a convex set
{\mathcal{U}}\subset\Upsilon(\rho_{0})
and
395\|{\mathtt{D}}_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}\|\leqslant\mu\sqrt{\lambda}

on
{\mathcal{E}}
. Then, on this event, it holds that

\left\|S\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\big)\right\|\leqslant\frac{16}{\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|^{2}\left(\lambda^{-1/4}+\sqrt{\frac{\left\|\Sigma-A^{*}\right\|}{\lambda}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|}{3\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right).

The proof of Lemma
6.1
is postponed to Section
D.2
.
Let us note that the first
d
components of the vector
S(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z})
coincide with
\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})
while the other entries of
S(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z})
are equal to zero. This yields that

\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\|=\left\|S(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z})\right\|.

Moreover, according to Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
, with probability at least
(1-3\delta/2)
, we simultaneously have

\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\rm F}\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},\quad\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(31)

and

\|{\bf U}\|\leqslant 8\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

(32)

We denote the corresponding event by
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
.
Then the inequalities
(
9
) ensure that

395\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\rm F}\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{2},\quad 395\|{\bf U}\|\leqslant\mu\sqrt{\lambda},\quad\text{and}\quad 395\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\lambda}}{2}.

Taking into account the triangle inequality
\|{\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z}\|\leqslant\|{\bf U}\|+\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
, we obtain that

\displaystyle 395^{2}\|{\mathtt{D}}_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{395^{2}}{2}\|{\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z}\|^{2}+395^{2}\mu^{2}\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{\mu^{2}\lambda}{2}\left(1+\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{2\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\mu^{2}\lambda}{4}\leqslant\mu^{2}\lambda.

Thus, the conditions of Lemma
6.1
are satisfied on the intersection of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
with
\mu_{0}=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{3}
. Due to the union bound
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{1})\geqslant 1-5\delta/2
. Moreover, applying Lemma
B.3
with
14\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|/\mu\leqslant 1/8
, we obtain that

\|A^{*}-\Sigma\|\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{64}+\frac{5\|\Sigma\|\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{16\mu}.

Since

\mu\sqrt{\lambda}\geqslant 8\cdot 18\cdot 56\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\quad\text{and}\quad\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|=\lambda\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\right\|\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|,

it holds that

\|A^{*}-\Sigma\|\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{64}+\frac{5\sqrt{\lambda}}{16\cdot 8\cdot 18\cdot 56}=\frac{4\sqrt{\lambda}}{16^{2}}+\frac{5\sqrt{\lambda}}{16^{2}\cdot 9\cdot 56}\leqslant\frac{9\sqrt{\lambda}}{16^{2}}.

Hence, we have just proved that the remainder
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|S\big(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\big)\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{16}{\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\,\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|^{2}\left(\lambda^{-1/4}+\frac{3\lambda^{-1/4}}{16}\right)\left(1+\frac{\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|}{3\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)

(33)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{19}{\mu_{0}\lambda^{3/4}}\,\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|^{2}\left(1+\frac{\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|}{3\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)

on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
of probability at least
(1-5\delta/2)
. We proceed with the analysis of
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
.

Step 3: bound on the norm of
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
.
The proof of the upper bound on
\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|
is based on a block representation of
\nabla^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
. Let
\chi=(\boldsymbol{\eta},A)\in\mathbb{R}^{d+d^{2}}
stand for the nuisance parameter. For any
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}=(\boldsymbol{\theta},\chi)=(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\eta},A)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d+d^{2}}
, we denote

H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),\quad H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}=H_{\chi\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\top}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}),\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\chi\chi}=\nabla_{\chi\chi}^{2}{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}).

With the introduced notations, the Hessian of
{\mathcal{L}}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})
is equal to

H=H(\boldsymbol{\upsilon})=\begin{pmatrix}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}&H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}\\
H_{\chi\boldsymbol{\theta}}&H_{\chi\chi}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}=H/H_{\chi\chi}
and
\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}=H/H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}
stand for the Schur complements of
H_{\chi\chi}
and
H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, respectively. Using the block-matrix inversion formula (see (
53
))

\begin{pmatrix}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}&H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}\\
H_{\chi\boldsymbol{\theta}}&H_{\chi\chi}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}&-\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\\
-\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}H_{\chi\boldsymbol{\theta}}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}&\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

and taking into account that
{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
has a form
({\bf 0},{\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi})
, where
{\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}=\big({\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z},\mu^{2}\,{\rm\bf vec}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\big)
,
we obtain that

H^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}=\begin{pmatrix}\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}&-\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\\
-\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}H_{\chi\boldsymbol{\theta}}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}&\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\\
\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}.\end{pmatrix}

(34)

Then, recalling the definition of
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}
(see (
30
)), we observe that

\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|^{2}=\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}(A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{1/2}\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\right\|^{2}+\left\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0\chi}\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\right\|^{2},

(35)

where we introduced

{\mathtt{D}}_{0\chi}^{2}={\rm diag}\big(2I_{d},I_{d}\otimes(\mu_{0}^{2}I_{d}+\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})^{\top})\big).

The two terms in the right-hand side of (
35
) should be studied separately. We start with the analysis of the subvector of
{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}
corresponding to the target parameter.

Lemma 6.2
.

Grant Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
and let
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\in\Upsilon(\rho)
for some
\rho\leqslant 1/5
(see (
19
) for the definition of
\Upsilon(\rho)
). Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and suppose that

{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)\leqslant n.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
be the event where (
31
) and (
32
) hold. Then there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
of probability at least
1-5\delta/2
such that

(i)

the following inequalities hold simultaneously on
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(36)

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(37)

\displaystyle|(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(38)

\displaystyle|(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(39)

\displaystyle\left|(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})^{\top}{\bf U}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\sigma\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}};

(40)

(ii)

on the intersection of
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}A^{\top}A+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{1/2}\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{1-10\rho^{2}}\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}A^{\top}A+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1/2}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{1-10\rho^{2}}\left(2\sigma+C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})\|+\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4\sqrt{2}\rho^{2}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{1-10\rho^{2}}\left(2\sigma+3C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8\rho\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\eta})\|}{(1-10\rho^{2})\mu\sqrt{\lambda}}\left(C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}\|+\sigma\right)\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

We provide the proof of Lemma
6.2
in Section
D.3
. Let us elaborate on the result of this lemma when
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}=\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
. According to Lemma
B.2
(see
(ii)
and
(iv)
), we have

\|A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*}\|\leqslant\sqrt{\lambda}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|,\quad\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|,

and

\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\leqslant\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|\left(1+\frac{3\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\left(1+\frac{3}{111}\right)=\frac{38\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{37}.

This means that
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
belongs to
\Upsilon(\rho^{*})
with
\rho^{*}=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|/\mu\leqslant 1/112
. Hence, there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}
such that
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*})\geqslant(1-5\delta/2)
and the inequalities

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(41)

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(42)

\displaystyle|(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(43)

\displaystyle|(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}|

\displaystyle\leqslant 4C_{X}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}},

(44)

\displaystyle\left|(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})^{\top}{\bf U}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\sigma\|\Sigma^{1/2}(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})\|\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

(45)

hold simultaneously on
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}
.
Moreover, on
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}(A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{1/2}\breve{H}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}){\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 1.001\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}(A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1/2}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}){\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}\right\|

(46)

and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\frac{1}{2}(A^{*})^{\top}A^{*}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1/2}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}){\bf\mathfrak{z}}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sqrt{2}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\left(2\sigma+\frac{38C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{37}+\sqrt{2}C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{4\sqrt{2}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{112^{2}}\left(2\sigma+3C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

(47)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{112^{2}}\left(C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|+\sigma\right)\sqrt{\frac{1+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(4\sqrt{2}+\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{112^{2}}+\frac{8}{112^{2}}\right)\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\left(\sigma+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

Finally, the next lemma concerns the norm of
({\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}})_{\chi}
and finishes the third step of the proof.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\rho\leqslant 1/16
and fix an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}\in\Upsilon(\rho)
, where
\Upsilon(\rho)
is defined in (
19
). Introduce

\widetilde{\mathtt{D}}^{2}={\rm diag}\left(2I_{d},I_{d}\otimes(\widetilde{\mu}^{2}I_{d}+\boldsymbol{\theta}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top})\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+d^{2})\times(d+d^{2})},

where
\widetilde{\mu}
is an arbitrary number from
[0,\mu]
. Finally, let
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
be the event where (
31
) and (
32
) hold. Then, on this event, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\mathtt{D}}\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(1+\frac{9\rho}{2}\right)\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\left(1+\frac{9\rho}{2}\right)C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\left(\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|^{2}}+\frac{9\rho\mu}{2}\right)C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

The proof of Lemma
6.3
is moved to Section
D.4
.
Similarly to Lemma
6.2
, we are going to apply Lemma
6.3
to
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}=\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
. As we discussed earlier,
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
lies in
\Upsilon(\rho)
, where
\rho^{*}=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|/\mu\leqslant 1/112
. Taking
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu_{0}=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{3}
,
\rho=\rho^{*}\leqslant 1/112
, and
\boldsymbol{\upsilon}=\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0\chi}\breve{H}_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(1+\frac{9}{224}\right)\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\left(1+\frac{9}{224}\right)C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+4\left(2\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|+\frac{9\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{224}\right)C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

(48)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{17}{2}\left(\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}.

on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
. Here we used the bound
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
following from the definition of
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
(see Lemma
B.2
(iv)
). The inequalities (
35
), (
46
), (
47
), and (
48
) immediately imply that

\displaystyle\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant 1.001\left(4\sqrt{2}+\frac{8\sqrt{2}}{112^{2}}+\frac{8}{112^{2}}\right)\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\left(\sigma+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+8\left(1+\frac{9}{224}\right)\left(\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 14\left(\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\right)\sqrt{\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}}\equiv\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}

on the intersection of the events
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
, and
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}
(that is, with probability at least
1-5\delta
). In view of (
33
), on the same event, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|S(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*}-H_{*}^{-1}\mathfrak{z})\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{19}{\mu_{0}\lambda^{3/4}}\,\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|^{2}\left(1+\frac{\|{\mathtt{D}}_{0}H_{*}^{-1}{\bf\mathfrak{z}}\|}{3\mu_{0}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)

(49)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{19\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3/4}}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\Psi(n,\delta)}{27\lambda}}\right)\Psi(n,\delta).

and (
29
) follows.

Step 4: elaborating on
{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
.

It remains to quantify the difference between
{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
and

\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\left(\Sigma({\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z})-\Sigma(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Sigma{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right).

We perform this task in three tiny steps. First, we note that
{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}
can be approximated by

-\left(\Sigma^{2}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}.

We support this claim by the following result.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume that the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
fulfil (
7
) and that
\mu\geqslant 112\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
. Then, on the event where (
47
) holds, we also have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}+\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\Sigma^{2}+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{2\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)},

where

\Psi(n,\delta)=196\left(\frac{\sigma\|\Sigma\|^{1/2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}+2C_{X}\|\Sigma\|\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)^{2}+\log(4/\delta)}{n}.

We postpone the proof of Lemma
6.4
to Section
D.5
and finish the proof of Theorem
2.4
first. The next auxiliary lemmata ensure that
H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}
is close to

-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Sigma({\bf Z}-\mathbb{E}{\bf Z})-\Sigma(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Sigma{\bf b}_{\lambda}\right).

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume that the parameters
\mu
and
\lambda
fulfil (
7
) and that
\mu\geqslant 112\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
. Then, on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}
, the vector

\boldsymbol{\tau}=(A^{*})^{\top}{\bf U}-(A^{*})^{\top}(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ})-2(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)(A^{*}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{*})

satisfies the inequality

\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\Sigma^{2}/2+\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\left(H_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\chi}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})H_{\chi\chi}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\upsilon}^{*})\mathfrak{z}_{\chi}+0.5\boldsymbol{\tau}\right)\right\|\leqslant\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|^{3}}{\mu^{2}\lambda^{1/4}}\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}.

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume that the parameters
\mu\geqslant 112\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|
and
\lambda>0
fulfil (
7
) and that
4n\geqslant{\mathtt{r}}(\Sigma)+\log(4/\delta)
. Let
\boldsymbol{\tau}
be as defined in Lemma
6.6
and let

\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\Sigma{\bf U}-\Sigma(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma){\bf b}_{\lambda}-(\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Sigma{\bf b}_{\lambda}.

Then there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}
of probability at least
(1-3\delta/2)
such that on
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{3}
, it holds that

\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\tau}-\boldsymbol{\zeta})\|\leqslant\frac{12\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{25\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}.

The proofs of Lemma
6.5
and Lemma
6.6
are moved to Sections
D.6
and
D.7
, respectively. Summing up Lemmata
6.4
,
6.5
, and
6.6
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}{\bf s}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{*}-\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{12}{25}+\frac{1}{17^{2}}\right)\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}

(50)

on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{2}^{*}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{3}
.
Finally, the triangle inequality and the bounds (
49
), (
50
), imply that, with probability at least
(1-13\delta/2)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}-\left(\Sigma^{2}+2\lambda I_{d}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{19\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3/4}}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\Psi(n,\delta)}{27\lambda}}\right)\Psi(n,\delta)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\lambda^{1/4}}\left(\left(\frac{17\|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\circ}\|}{\mu}\right)^{2}+\frac{\|{\bf b}_{\lambda}\|}{160\mu}\right)\sqrt{\Psi(n,\delta)}.

\square

Appendix A
Auxiliary results: properties of the Hessian of
